40. Fullscreen toggle on all three 3D model viewers
  Person's request, after a "just a question" about whether the crane
  layout 3D view could go fullscreen: yes, and add it to every 3D viewer
  in the app, not just that one - carrier-3d-wrap (Support Pad
  Placement), carrier-3d-wrap-layout (Crane Layout), cwt-3d-wrap (CWT
  Combinations).
  Deliberately fullscreens the OUTER wrap div, not the canvas directly -
  both carrier3d.js and cwt3d.js already listen for window's own 'resize'
  event and read their inner canvas-wrap's clientWidth/clientHeight on
  every fire (see each module's own resizeRenderer()), which the browser
  fires automatically on entering/exiting fullscreen - so the renderer/
  camera correctly resize to fill the screen with ZERO changes needed to
  either module. This is only a DOM/Fullscreen API + CSS + a small shared
  toggle function in index.html's own classic script - no CARRIER3D_
  VERSION/CWT3D_VERSION bump needed, matching the convention that those
  only bump when the vendor files themselves change.
  One button per wrap, all sharing the same .carrier3d-fullscreen-btn
  class/markup (same generic multi-instance pattern already used for the
  offline-cache buttons) - positioned top-right inside the wrap (label
  overlay already owns top-left), so it's reachable both in and out of
  fullscreen without a separate fullscreen-only control. Swaps between a
  Feather-style expand/compress SVG pair on 'fullscreenchange'/
  'webkitfullscreenchange', keyed by comparing document.fullscreenElement
  against each button's own data-wrap attribute (so only the button whose
  own wrap is actually fullscreened swaps its icon, not all three at
  once).
  Hidden by default and only revealed once the Fullscreen API is
  feature-detected as available (document.documentElement.
  requestFullscreen) - iOS Safari on iPhone has never supported
  fullscreening an arbitrary element (video-only), so the button simply
  doesn't appear there rather than doing nothing when tapped, same
  feature-detect-then-reveal pattern already used for the AR button.
  Verified via Playwright: all three buttons correctly reveal
  (feature-detected as supported in headless Chromium); calling the
  toggle function actually engaged real fullscreen (document.
  fullscreenElement became the target wrap, confirmed via a screenshot
  showing the model filling the full viewport with the button correctly
  positioned top-right); 0 console errors. One thing NOT fully verified
  in this environment: the icon swap fires correctly when called
  directly, but the 'fullscreenchange' event itself didn't fire when
  fullscreen was triggered via Playwright's programmatic page.evaluate()
  call rather than a genuine trusted user click - a known automation-
  environment quirk (requestFullscreen succeeded regardless, so the
  underlying API call itself is fine), not a code defect, since real
  browsers reliably fire fullscreenchange for a real user tap. Worth a
  quick on-device glance to confirm the icon actually flips when tapped
  for real.
  CACHE_VERSION v154->v155, app version v3.24->v3.25.

  Person's request: "Hook Block Fitted" should default to that crane's

  NAME against the same FLEET_BLOCK_SPEC catalog already used elsewhere
  (Minimum Hook Block Weights tab) rather than hardcoding per-crane

  exactly one block whose name contains "3-Sheave" (confirmed by
  reading every entry), so that's the main block's default with no
  per-crane exception needed. Winch 2 prefers a block whose name
  contains "Load Hook" - only 3 of the 7 cranes actually stock one in
  this catalog (1650-t3, 1300, 1100); the other 4 (1250/1220[unused by
  this tab]/1160/1130/1110) fall back to the LIGHTEST block available
  instead of being left on an arbitrary first entry - the closest real
  equivalent to "just a hook" when the catalog has nothing plainer.
  Verified every crane's resulting default against the real catalog by
  hand before trusting it (both defaults, all 7 cranes) - full table
  matched exactly.
  Found and fixed a real latent bug while testing this: onLoadChartCrane
  Change()'s fetch had no guard against a stale response landing after
  a newer one - switching cranes fast enough (well beyond normal click
  speed, only surfaced by automated rapid-fire testing) could let an
  in-flight fetch for the PREVIOUS crane resolve after the new one and
  silently overwrite its already-correct render, including these new
  block defaults, with stale data. Added a one-line guard (bail if
  document.getElementById('lc-crane').value no longer matches the
  crane this fetch was actually for) - pre-existing bug, not introduced
  by this change, but directly threatened the new feature's reliability
  so fixed alongside it rather than filed away.
  Verified via Playwright: single fresh-page-load check per crane
  (isolates the defaulting logic itself) - all 7 correct. Rapid
  sequential crane-switching on one page (the scenario that exposed the

  "12.5t Single Load Hook (0.50 t)" as the Winch 2 Block Fitted default.

46. LRT 1100: "move forward" looked like it moved backward

  Person's report: on Support Pad Placement, shifting the LRT 1100
  "forward" visibly moved it backward - works correctly for every other
  crane. Given methodology.txt 45's frontAtMinZ:false determination was
  the one genuinely uncertain part of last session's LRT 1100 work, this
  read at first like that guess might simply be wrong and needed
  re-checking from scratch.

  It wasn't. Re-verified frontAtMinZ:false from three more independent
  angles before touching any code: (1) the actual OEM LICCON button
  labels, straight from the operator manual text ("Select support 3 front
  left" / "support 2 front right" / "support 4 rear left" / "support 1
  rear right") - matches cadFleetData's existing C1_RR/C2_FR/C3_FL/C4_RL
  assignment exactly, so the leg data (separate from the 3D calibration)
  was never in question; (2) the manual's own Fig.145199 elevation
  drawing, which shows a real windshield-and-door cab (not just an engine
  box) sitting at the same end as the "4190" counterweight-radius
  dimension chain and the boom base - i.e. the true front; (3) rebuilt the
  calcCAD() math for a 10m forward shift by hand and confirmed C1 (a real
  rear-right leg, angle 43 deg) lands at 149.5 deg after the shift -
  correctly swung into the front-angle range. Playwright-tested the 2D
  Visual Site Plan side-by-side against LTM 1110 for the identical 10m
  shift - pixel-for-pixel the same behavior, target cluster and "FRONT"
  label both land on the correct side for both cranes. The underlying
  math, leg data, and 2D plan were never the bug.

  The actual bug: the 3D preview's DEFAULT camera angle (frameCamera(),
  shown the instant a carrier model opens, before anyone clicks Fit View)
  was a fixed local-space offset - always +Z in the model's own raw
  export coordinates, never adjusted for calibration. For the other six
  carriers (all frontAtMinZ:true, all exported with front at local min-Z
  by whatever consistent convention their CAD source used), that fixed
  +Z offset happens to sit on each model's REAR side, so every one of
  them opens rear-first: cab small and distant, rear axles filling the
  foreground - confirmed by rendering LTM 1110's own default view before
  touching anything, which showed exactly that. LRT 1100 is the one
  carrier whose raw export has front at local MAX-Z instead (a real
  difference in this particular Onshape export, not a mistake - see
  methodology.txt 45) - so that same fixed +Z offset lands on ITS front
  side, and the crane opened nose-on, cab filling the foreground, unlike
  every other crane in the fleet. A forward shift was always moving the
  ghost/target position toward the true front - but with the camera
  planted in front of the crane instead of behind it like normal, that
  motion reads on screen as growing toward the viewer instead of
  receding, which feels exactly like "backward" even though the
  underlying position was always correct. Confirmed by rendering LRT
  1100's own default view too: ladders/cab structure filled the
  foreground, opposite of LTM 1110.

  Fix: frameCamera(root, calibration) now flips the Z offset by the same
  dirSign convention computeFormulaCalibration already uses (frontAtMinZ
  true -> +1, false -> -1), so the initial camera always lands on the
  site-space rear side regardless of which end of the raw GLB happens to
  sit at local +Z. __carrier3dActivate gained a 5th (optional) calibration
  parameter to carry this through from both call sites in index.html
  (activateCarrier3D, activateCarrier3DLayout), each already holding
  CARRIER_CALIBRATION[modelKey] in scope. Fit View (fitView(), the
  near-top-down button) was never affected - its tilt is 0.5 degrees off
  vertical, functionally camera-angle-agnostic regardless of which side
  it's nominally biased toward.

  Re-verified via Playwright after the fix: LRT 1100's default view now
  opens rear-first, ladders/cab small and distant - matching LTM 1110's
  own default view convention exactly. No other crane's default view
  changed (dirSign is +1 for all six of them, so the fix is a no-op for
  every carrier except this one).

  CARRIER3D_VERSION 47->48 (vendor/carrier3d.js changed). index.html also
  changed (the two call-site edits passing calibration through), so
  CACHE_VERSION and the app version both bump too, same as any other
  index.html change.

================================================================================
47. LRT 1100: frontAtMinZ was actually wrong, not just the camera

  methodology.txt 46's camera-angle fix made the default 3D view
  consistent again, but the person came back with the real root cause
  directly: CARRIER_CALIBRATION's frontAtMinZ: false for the LRT 1100 was
  itself backward - what methodology.txt 45 identified as the front (the
  MAX-Z end's raised, ladder-flanked structure) is actually the rear.
  Concrete symptom that proved it: the "Show ground layout marks" 3D
  overlay's own half-carrier-width dimension line - explicitly labelled
  "(rear, C1 side)" in the plain-text Layout Dimensions card - was
  rendering at the FRONT end of the model instead, on live testing.

  Root cause of the original wrong read: this crane's operator manual
  lists "Crane cab" as a component of "2 Crane superstructure", not of
  "1 Crane chassis" (chapter 1.01 Terminology, Fig.146075 vs Fig.146074) -
  i.e. on an LRT (rough-terrain) crane the only cab is mounted on the
  ROTATING SUPERSTRUCTURE, used for both driving and operating, not fixed
  to the carrier. The carrier-only GLB this app renders has no cab on it
  at all. The raised, ladder-flanked structure methodology.txt 45 found at
  the MAX-Z end and read as "classic driver's-cab access" was something
  else entirely - almost certainly the engine bay (a real carrier
  component per Fig.146074's own parts list, item 5) - which happens to
  sit at the carrier's REAR, not front, on this particular machine.
  Everything methodology.txt 45 cross-checked against (cadFleetData's
  C1-C4 leg assignment, the literal OEM LICCON button labels, the
  counterweight-radius clearance figure) was and remains correct in its
  own right - none of that data was wrong. The mistake was narrower: which
  physical end of the 3D model those correct front/rear labels belonged
  to. The "independent" cross-check in methodology.txt 45 wasn't actually
  independent of the error - it rendered its own confirmation using the
  same (wrong) calibration it was trying to verify, so it validated
  internal self-consistency, not truth against the real machine. Lesson:
  a cross-check that itself depends on the value being checked can't
  catch that value being wrong.

  Fix: CARRIER_CALIBRATION['1100'].frontAtMinZ flipped false -> true -
  same convention every other carrier in the fleet already uses. Nothing
  else needed changing: FOOTPRINTS, cadFleetData, GROUND_LAYOUT_DATA and
  SLEW_CLEARANCE_DATA were all already correct (they were never derived
  from the 3D model's Z-axis guess in the first place), and
  computeFormulaCalibration/siteToWorld's dirSign compensation (see
  vendor/carrier3d.js) was already correctly designed to handle either
  polarity - it just needed the right input. methodology.txt 46's camera
  fix stays in place (still correct, still a no-op for every crane except
  one calibrated with frontAtMinZ: false - currently none, after this
  fix, but worth keeping for whatever crane needs it next).

  Re-verified via Playwright: the ground-layout half-width dimension line
  now renders at the C1/rear end as labelled; Support Pad Placement's 3D
  "if moved" ghost for a forward shift now lands near the correct
  (min-Z) end; the 3D default view still opens rear-first, unchanged from
  methodology.txt 46's fix (dirSign is now +1 for this crane too, so that
  fix is currently a no-op here, by design). 0 console errors.

================================================================================
48. LRT 1100: methodology.txt 47 was itself wrong - reverted

  Person sent their own reference photos of the real machine, one end at
  a time, plainly labelled "front" and "rear". The "front" photo shows
  the MAX-Z end - bore, raised ladder-flanked structure, stepped platform
  - the exact same end methodology.txt 45 originally (and correctly)
  called the front, before methodology.txt 47 flipped it. The "rear"
  photo shows the plain MIN-Z end. methodology.txt 47's theory (cab lives
  on the superstructure, so the carrier model can't have one, so the
  structure found must be something else at the other end) was a
  plausible-sounding chain of reasoning built without ever actually
  confirming which end the person's original complaint was about -
  wrong conclusion from a real fact (this crane's cab genuinely is
  superstructure-mounted) stretched into an unsupported one (therefore
  the carrier's own front must be the other end).

  Reverted CARRIER_CALIBRATION['1100'].frontAtMinZ back to false, the
  original methodology.txt 45 value. methodology.txt 46's camera-
  consistency fix is untouched and still correct - that was always a
  separate, genuinely real bug (the default view opening nose-on instead
  of rear-first) and is very likely what the person's original "half
  width dimensions on the front" report was actually seeing: a confusing
  default camera angle, not a wrong calibration value. Best current
  understanding is the ground-layout half-width dimension line was
  probably already rendering at the geometrically correct (rear, C1 side)
  end all along under frontAtMinZ: false - methodology.txt 47 never
  actually screenshotted the "before" state to confirm the reported
  symptom before changing the value, only reasoned from the report
  itself; that gap is exactly how a plausible wrong fix got shipped.
  Re-verified via Playwright after reverting: default 3D view still
  opens rear-first (methodology.txt 46 intact), 0 console errors.

  Lesson for next time a front/rear report comes in on any crane: get a
  screenshot of the actual current (allegedly wrong) state FIRST, before
  reasoning toward a fix - a mid-air diagnosis, however internally
  consistent it seems, isn't a substitute for looking at what's actually
  on screen when it's this cheap to just look.

================================================================================
49. LRT 1100: methodology.txt 48 was ALSO wrong - the original 45 render
    had the two ends mislabelled at the source

  Person came back a third time with concrete, specific evidence: "C1 is
  in C2 pos and vice versa, C4 and C3 are in each other's spot, half
  width dimensions still on the front, needs to move to the rear" - then
  sent an annotated screenshot of the app's own ground-layout-marks view
  with C3/C2 hand-labelled "front" (top) and C4/C1 hand-labelled "rear"
  (bottom).

  This time, instead of re-reasoning from the existing (evidently
  unreliable) close-up renders, rebuilt the identification from scratch
  with a throwaway page (not the live app - a bare Three.js scene loading
  ONLY outrigger/models/lrt1100-carrier.glb directly, full manual camera
  control, no OrbitControls polar-angle limits, no possibility of the
  live app's own calibration influencing what's on screen) and rendered
  genuine close-ups of both ends. Result: MIN-Z has a real ladder (clear
  rungs), an oval mirror/sensor disc, and a stepped platform. MAX-Z has a
  plain louvred radiator/engine grille - no ladder, nothing cab-adjacent
  about it. This is the OPPOSITE of what methodology.txt 45 originally
  reported ("MAX-Z has the raised, ladder-flanked structure"). Comparing
  both fresh renders directly against the person's own two reference
  photos from earlier in this saga: their "front" photo (bore, disc,
  ladder, stepped platform) matches the MIN-Z render exactly, feature for
  feature - not the MAX-Z one. methodology.txt 45's very first close-up
  pass had the two ends' renders mixed up at the source - a mistake in
  that one ad-hoc script, not a genuine ambiguity in the model - and
  every subsequent round this session (46 kept it, 47 correctly sensed
  something was wrong but landed on the wrong theory to explain it, 48
  reverted back to the original wrong value on the strength of photos
  that, on this re-examination, actually confirm the OPPOSITE) inherited
  that one original mislabelling.

  Fix: CARRIER_CALIBRATION['1100'].frontAtMinZ back to true - the third
  flip this session (45: false, 47: true, 48: false, 49: true). Re-
  verified by rendering the live app's own actual ground-layout-marks
  view (not just the throwaway comparison) and confirming pixel-for-pixel
  agreement with the person's annotated screenshot: C3/C2 at the top
  (front), C4/C1 at the bottom (rear), half-width dimension now at the
  rear. 0 console errors.

  Why trust this one over the previous three: this is the first round
  built on a direct side-by-side of a labelled reference photo against a
  freshly, independently rendered close-up of each end - not on
  describing what a structure "reads like" from a screenshot that could
  itself have been mislabelled, and not on reasoning about which theory
  sounds more plausible. Every earlier round in this saga skipped
  straight to reasoning about calibration flags without first re-
  deriving the one fact everything else depends on: which physical end
  is which. That's the actual lesson here, sharper than
  methodology.txt 48's "look before reasoning" - it's specifically LOOK
  AT THE RAW MODEL AGAIN FROM SCRATCH before trusting any earlier
  session's own claimed visual identification, because that identifi-
  cation is exactly the kind of thing that can be silently wrong at the
  source and then get treated as settled fact by everything downstream.

50. CWT Combinations: auto-scroll to centre on switching to 3D

  Person's request: clicking "3D Model" on the Counterweight Stack card
  should bring that card front-and-centre in the viewport, not just flip
  the toggle and leave the page wherever it happened to be scrolled -
  same UX carrier3d.js's own Fit View button already provides (see its
  own comment: on a phone the card can sit mostly below the fold, so a
  view change alone can leave a person still having to scroll manually to
  see it). CWT's 3D view doesn't have a Fit View button of its own to
  piggyback on, so this fires directly from setCwtView('3d') instead:
  `document.getElementById('cwt-stack-card').scrollIntoView({ behavior:
  'smooth', block: 'center' })`. Scrolls the whole card (toggle buttons,
  viewer, hint text, total-weight readout) rather than just the 3D wrap
  div, so the person lands on the complete context, not just the model
  floating with no surrounding UI visible. Only fires switching TO 3D,
  not away from it - matches "when I click the 3d model tab" specifically.
  Verified via Playwright at both a laptop-ish viewport and a phone-sized
  one (390x844): card's own vertical centre lands within a pixel of the
  viewport's centre in both, 0 console errors.

================================================================================
51. Chain Shortening: "Mark Cut Point in AR"

  Person's own idea, following an earlier exploratory chat about AR
  measurement in general: given a computed result like "remove 54 links /
  2340mm", could AR show exactly where the 54th link physically falls
  from a chosen starting link, rather than the person having to count
  links by eye or trust a tape measure end staying put on a hanging
  chain?

  Ruled out up front: true "point the camera at the chain, it recognises
  and highlights link 54 automatically" needs reliable per-link computer
  vision (identify, segment and count individual chain links across
  lighting/angle/chain type) - not realistic with what's available here,
  and would be flaky even attempted. What IS genuinely buildable, and
  what got built: tap the reference link in AR (real-world hit-test
  placement, not CV), tap a second point further along the same chain to
  set direction, and get a marker placed at the exact real-world distance
  computed by the calculator (links-to-remove x pitch) along that
  direction - the person then counts/aligns the physical chain against a
  known-correct point in space, rather than trusting an unsupported tape
  measure to stay put or hold a straight line down a hanging/coiled
  chain.

  Built as a new standalone module, vendor/armeasure.js, deliberately NOT
  folded into carrier3d.js despite reusing its WebXR AR patterns
  (immersive-ar session request with hit-test + optional dom-overlay,
  ring reticle, canvas-texture text sprite, session lifecycle/cleanup) -
  this feature has no GLB model to load and no orbit-preview mode, so
  pulling in carrier3d.js's GLTFLoader/DRACOLoader/OrbitControls would
  only add dead weight neither this file nor its first-load cost needs.
  Same dynamic-import-on-first-use convention as cwt3d.js/carrier3d.js -
  never fetched until "Mark Cut Point in AR" is actually pressed.

  Wired into the Chain Shortening tab: calcChainShortening() now also
  calls setChainARResult(distanceMm, links) on every recalculation -
  distanceMm is always "links to remove x pitch" (the physical amount of
  chain being removed), the same real quantity regardless of which input
  mode (Current & Desired Length vs Shorten By Amount) produced it, so
  one code path covers both. The button is feature-detected exactly like
  every other AR entry point in this app (updateChainARButtonVisibility,
  same navigator.xr.isSessionSupported('immersive-ar') check as
  updateARButtonVisibility) and additionally hidden whenever there's no
  valid result yet or links-to-remove rounds to 0 (nothing meaningful to
  mark). Added to allOfflineContentUrls() too (armeasure.js + the shared
  three.js core files, deduped against the two existing viewer bundles'
  own three.js entries) so "Cache All Offline Content" covers it the same
  as every other fetched-on-demand asset.

  Verified via Playwright (real WebXR hardware isn't available in this
  environment, same limitation the original carrier3d.js AR feature was
  shipped under - see its own "present-but-hidden, feature-detected"
  testing note): button correctly hidden with no navigator.xr; correctly
  shown once navigator.xr.isSessionSupported is mocked to resolve true;
  clicking it triggers the dynamic import, computes the right label from
  the live calculator result (confirmed against the exact numbers shown
  in the result table), and calls requestSession with the correct options
  (requiredFeatures: ['hit-test'], optionalFeatures: ['dom-overlay'], a
  real dom-overlay root) - checked directly by capturing the call rather
  than assuming it; the on-screen AR overlay renders with the correct
  first-tap prompt. A session-request failure (mocked, since a fully
  WebXR-compliant fake session object is impractical to construct without
  real hardware) is caught and logged without any uncaught exception -
  confirmed pageerror stayed empty separately from the expected
  console.error. allOfflineContentUrls() confirmed to include the new
  file without throwing. 0 uncaught page errors across every test.
  ARMEASURE_VERSION starts at 1.
